The combination of grief, guilt over not being present for his family, and the daily exposure to patients with cardiac and terminal illnesses at work created a constant backdrop of loss that he tried to manage by working longer hours and volunteering at more community health fairs. As the months passed, close colleagues noticed that his usual balance between structured care and self-care had collapsed: he stopped joining hikes, let his photography and cooking fall away, and increasingly kept to himself even during breaks, seeming preoccupied and withdrawn. He finally agreed to seek psychiatric help after confiding to a fellow physician that he was convinced his patients would be better off without him and had started to dwell repeatedly on the idea that he would be \"relieved\" if he simply did not wake up, prompting his colleague to insist on an urgent evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “I keep thinking everyone would be better off if I just weren’t here anymore, and these thoughts about not wanting to wake up won’t stop.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S019 (Delusions): I know it sounds irrational, but I'm absolutely convinced that my dad died because I wasn’t there, like somehow my absence caused his heart attack, and now I feel the hospital and even my patients are silently blaming me and waiting for me to make another fatal mistake to prove it.\n- S020 (Hallucinations): When I’m alone in the call room or driving home late, I sometimes hear my father’s voice, clear as day, telling me I failed him and that my patients will die because of me, even though I know logically he would never say that when he was alive.\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to look forward to hiking, shooting photos on weekends, even trying new recipes, but for months now those things feel completely flat and pointless, and I usually just sit in my apartment staring at the wall or scrolling my phone without caring about anything.\n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): Lately even simple things like getting from my car to the clinic feel like I’m moving through wet cement—my body is heavy, my steps are slow, and nurses have started asking if I’m okay because I just stand there staring at charts before I can make myself move.\n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): Most days I feel like a fraud who doesn’t deserve to be a doctor or a son, and I get stuck replaying every missed phone call to my dad, every minor error at work, convinced that I’m fundamentally a bad person who should be punished for all the harm I’ve caused.\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): On rounds I find myself reading the same line in a chart over and over and still not absorbing it, and even choosing what to eat for lunch can take me ten minutes because my mind feels foggy and I can’t seem to decide anything.\n- S032 (Death_Suicide): For months now I’ve gone to bed thinking it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up, and on the worst days I catch myself picturing specific ways I could end my life, like driving into a barrier on the highway, even though I haven’t acted on it.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- delusions_and_hallucinations_occur_exclusively_during_the_major_depressive_episode: Those thoughts that my absence killed my dad and the times I hear his voice accusing me only started after I sank into this deep depression after his death, and on the rare days when my mood briefly lifts a bit, those voices and fixed beliefs about being to blame fade into the background or aren’t there at all.\n- functional_impairment: Over the past year I’ve gone from being one of the more engaged physicians on my team to barely keeping up with notes and follow-ups, avoiding colleagues, and letting basic things like cooking, laundry, and cleaning slide to the point that my apartment and my work performance both feel like they’re falling apart.\n- duration: These problems have been going on These feelings only began after my father's death a little over a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Since these feelings began, have you felt physically slowed down, like your movements or speech are sluggish, or conversely, have you felt so restless that you cannot sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel incredibly slowed down, like I'm constantly trying to move through wet cement and my limbs are made of lead. It takes so much effort just to walk, and the nurses at the clinic have even asked if I'm okay because I'll just stand there staring at a chart before I can bring myself to move.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current feelings are primarily a reaction to the distress of losing your father, or do you feel that your symptoms have evolved beyond that specific event into a more generalized, pervasive state of depression?\n\nPatient: It definitely started with the grief and guilt over my father's death, but it has evolved into something much worse and more pervasive. Now, it feels like a constant, heavy fog that has taken over my entire life, affecting my work and self-care and leaving me feeling completely frozen and hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the feelings of sadness and hopelessness, have you experienced a persistent loss of interest or pleasure in almost all of the activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, completely. Hobbies I used to love like hiking, photography, and cooking now feel entirely flat and pointless. Now, I mostly just spend my free time staring at the wall or aimlessly scrolling through my phone without caring about anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of hopelessness and your inability to function are constant throughout the day, or do you have moments where your mood lifts significantly when something positive happens?\n\nPatient: For the most part, this heavy hopelessness and inability to function have been constant and steady every day for over a year. There are very rare days when my mood briefly lifts a bit, and during those times, the voices of my dad and the intense guilt fade into the background. But overall, those moments are extremely rare and the heaviness is almost always there throughout the day."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
